MC	<p>1955_1420</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- GCGAACTTT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>b32e_81ea</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- ATGTCTGTC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; border: 1px solid #999; display:inline-table;"> <tr><td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;">3&prime;&ndash;</td><td style="border: 1px solid #999; MC	<p>7fc5_d011</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- AGGTTAGTC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>563e_ba18</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TAAGAAGCG --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>d539_5dd1</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. MC	<p>4e57_e9a5</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- CCTCCGTAT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>3594_442f</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- GCTTACCGA --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; border: 1px solid #999; display:inline-table;"> <tr><td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;">3&prime;&ndash;</td><td style="border: 1px solid #999; MC	<p>287a_a540</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- CAATGCGAT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; border: 1px solid #999; display:inline-table;"> <tr><td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;">3&prime;&ndash;</td><td style="border: 1px solid #999; MC	<p>74a1_b3c9</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- GGTCTCTGA --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>307f_158b</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. MC	<p>4be5_112a</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TCAGGTTAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; border: 1px solid #999; display:inline-table;"> <tr><td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;">3&prime;&ndash;</td><td style="border: 1px solid #999; MC	<p>b7ce_3d90</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- GTGGTTAGT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>22c1_d475</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TGACCTCAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>38f6_23ed</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- GTTCTATCT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>8e2a_4f65</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TTGTAGGTG --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>ca4c_d3fc</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- CACGTGTAA --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>fee2_409f</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- GAGCATTGT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>316f_80a9</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. MC	<p>c884_a25f</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- ACGACACTC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; border: 1px solid #999; display:inline-table;"> <tr><td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;">3&prime;&ndash;</td><td style="border: 1px solid #999; MC	<p>bb7f_e34d</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- ATTCGACAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse;
